</div>

<div id="mwAkM" class="section" mw-section-id="17"
aria-labelledby="Higher-order_curves">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Higher-order curves

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Higher-order curves")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

For higher-order curves one needs correspondingly more intermediate
points. For cubic curves one can construct intermediate points
**Q**<sub>0</sub>, **Q**<sub>1</sub>, and **Q**<sub>2</sub> that
describe linear Bézier curves, and points **R**<sub>0</sub> and
**R**<sub>1</sub> that describe quadratic Bézier curves:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| <span id="mwAlM" typeof="mw:File" mw="{&quot;caption&quot;:&quot;Construction of a cubic Bézier curve&quot;}"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_3_big.svg" id="mwAlQ"
class="mw-file-description"
title="Construction of a cubic Bézier curve"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/B%C3%A9zier_3_big.svg/250px-B%C3%A9zier_3_big.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"
id="mwAlU" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_3_big.svg"
decoding="async" data-file-width="360" data-file-height="150"
data-file-type="drawing"
srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/89/B%C3%A9zier_3_big.svg/500px-B%C3%A9zier_3_big.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x"
width="240" height="100"
alt="Construction of a cubic Bézier curve" /></a></span> |  | <span id="mwAlk" typeof="mw:File" mw="{&quot;caption&quot;:&quot;Animation of a cubic Bézier curve, &lt;i id=\&quot;mwAlg\&quot;&gt;t&lt;/i&gt; in [0,1]&quot;}"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_3_big.gif" id="mwAlo"
class="mw-file-description"
title="Animation of a cubic Bézier curve, t in [0,1]"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/B%C3%A9zier_3_big.gif/250px-B%C3%A9zier_3_big.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"
id="mwAls" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_3_big.gif"
decoding="async" data-file-width="360" data-file-height="150"
data-file-type="bitmap"
srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/db/B%C3%A9zier_3_big.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled 2x"
width="240" height="100"
alt="Animation of a cubic Bézier curve, t in [0,1]" /></a></span> |
| Construction of a cubic Bézier curve |  | Animation of a cubic Bézier curve, *t* in \[0,1\] |

For fourth-order curves one can construct intermediate points
**Q**<sub>0</sub>, **Q**<sub>1</sub>, **Q**<sub>2</sub> and
**Q**<sub>3</sub> that describe linear Bézier curves, points
**R**<sub>0</sub>, **R**<sub>1</sub> and **R**<sub>2</sub> that describe
quadratic Bézier curves, and points **S**<sub>0</sub> and
**S**<sub>1</sub> that describe cubic Bézier curves:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| <span id="mwAng" typeof="mw:File" mw="{&quot;caption&quot;:&quot;Construction of a quartic Bézier curve&quot;}"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_4_big.svg" id="mwAnk"
class="mw-file-description"
title="Construction of a quartic Bézier curve"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/B%C3%A9zier_4_big.svg/250px-B%C3%A9zier_4_big.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"
id="mwAno" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_4_big.svg"

width="240" height="100"
alt="Construction of a quartic Bézier curve" /></a></span> |  | <span id="mwAn4" typeof="mw:File" mw="{&quot;caption&quot;:&quot;Animation of a quartic Bézier curve, &lt;i id=\&quot;mwAn0\&quot;&gt;t&lt;/i&gt; in [0,1]&quot;}"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_4_big.gif" id="mwAn8"
class="mw-file-description"
title="Animation of a quartic Bézier curve, t in [0,1]"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/B%C3%A9zier_4_big.gif/250px-B%C3%A9zier_4_big.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"

width="240" height="100"
alt="Animation of a quartic Bézier curve, t in [0,1]" /></a></span> |
| Construction of a quartic Bézier curve |  | Animation of a quartic Bézier curve, *t* in \[0,1\] |

For fifth-order curves, one can construct similar intermediate points.

|  |
|----|
| <span id="mwAos" typeof="mw:File" mw="{&quot;caption&quot;:&quot;Animation of the construction of a fifth-order Bézier curve&quot;}"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BezierCurve.gif" id="mwAow"
class="mw-file-description"
title="Animation of the construction of a fifth-order Bézier curve"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/BezierCurve.gif/250px-BezierCurve.gif?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"
id="mwAo0" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:BezierCurve.gif"
decoding="async" data-file-width="473" data-file-height="378"

width="240" height="192"

These representations rest on the process used in
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Casteljau&#39;s_algorithm"
id="mwApI" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="De Casteljau&#39;s algorithm">De
Casteljau's algorithm</a> to calculate Bézier
curves.<sup><a href="#cite_note-17" id="mwApM"><span id="mwApQ"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwApU"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>15<span id="mwApY"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

</div>

<div id="mwApc" class="section" mw-section-id="18"
aria-labelledby="Offsets_(or_stroking)_of_Bézier_curves">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### <span id="Offsets_.28or_stroking.29_of_B.C3.A9zier_curves" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Offsets (or stroking) of Bézier curves

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Offsets (or stroking) of Bézier curves")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

The curve at a fixed offset from a given Bézier curve, called an
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parallel_curve" id="mwApk"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Parallel curve">offset or parallel curve</a> in
mathematics (lying "parallel" to the original curve, like the offset
between rails in a
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Railroad_track" id="mwApo"
class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Railroad track">railroad
track</a>), cannot be exactly formed by a Bézier curve (except in some
trivial cases). In general, the two-sided offset curve of a cubic Bézier
is a 10th-order
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algebraic_curve" id="mwAps"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Algebraic curve">algebraic curve</a><sup><a href="#cite_note-Kilgard-18" id="mwApw"><span id="mwAp0"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAp4"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>16<span id="mwAp8"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> and more generally for a
Bézier of degree *n* the two-sided offset curve is an algebraic curve of
degree 4*n*<span id="mwAqI" typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>−<span id="mwAqM"
typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>2.<sup><a href="#cite_note-19" id="mwAqQ"><span id="mwAqU"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAqY"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>17<span id="mwAqc"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> However, there are
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heuristic" id="mwAqg"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Heuristic">heuristic</a> methods that usually
give an adequate approximation for practical
purposes.<sup><a href="#cite_note-20" id="mwAqk"><span id="mwAqo"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAqs"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>18<span id="mwAqw"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

In the field of
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vector_graphics" id="mwAq4"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Vector graphics">vector graphics</a>, painting
two symmetrically distanced offset curves is called *stroking* (the
Bézier curve or in general a path of several Bézier
segments).<sup><a href="#cite_note-Kilgard-18" id="mwArA"><span id="mwArE"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwArI"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>16<span id="mwArM"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> The conversion from
offset curves to filled Bézier contours is of practical importance in
converting <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Font" id="mwArQ"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Font">fonts</a> defined in
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metafont" id="mwArU"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Metafont">Metafont</a>, which require stroking
of Bézier curves, to the more widely used
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PostScript_fonts" id="mwArY"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="PostScript fonts">PostScript type 1 fonts</a>,
which only require (for efficiency purposes) the mathematically simpler
operation of filling a contour defined by (non-self-intersecting) Bézier
curves.<sup><a href="#cite_note-21" id="mwArc"><span id="mwArg"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwArk"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>19<span id="mwAro"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

</div>

</div>

<div id="mwArs" class="section" mw-section-id="19"
aria-labelledby="Degree_elevation">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2">

## Degree elevation

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Degree elevation")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

A Bézier curve of degree *n* can be converted into a Bézier curve of
degree *n*<span id="mwAr8" typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>+ 1 *with the same
shape*. This is useful if software supports Bézier curves only of
specific degree. For example, systems that can only work with cubic
Bézier curves can implicitly work with quadratic curves by using their
equivalent cubic representation.

To do degree elevation, we use the equality <span id="mwAsI"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt118"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{B}(t) = (1-t)\\mathbf{B}(t) + t\\mathbf{B}(t).&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {B} (t)=(1-t)\mathbf {B} (t)+t\mathbf {B} (t).}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e86d4e966277735c34735fb2ad06d46af2b07cbe"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.838ex; width:27.727ex; height:2.843ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {B} (t)=(1-t)\mathbf {B} (t)+t\mathbf {B} (t).}" /></span>
Each component <span id="mwAsM"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt119"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{b}_{i,n}(t)\\mathbf{P}_i&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {b} _{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/90e158158bc1c7b8e6d14a839588d75087a1b662"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -1.005ex; width:9.004ex; height:3.009ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {b} _{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}}" /></span> is
multiplied by (1<span id="mwAsQ"
typeof="mw:Entity"> </span><span id="mwAsU"
typeof="mw:Entity">−</span><span id="mwAsY"
typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>*t*) and<span id="mwAsg"
typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>*t*, thus increasing a degree by one, without
changing the value. Here is the example of increasing degree from 2
to<span id="mwAso" typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>3.

<span id="mwAs0" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt120"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\begin{align}\n(1 - t)^2 \\mathbf{P}_0 + 2(1 - t)t\\mathbf{P}_1 + t^2 \\mathbf{P}_2 &amp;= (1 - t)^3 \\mathbf{P}_0 + 2(1 - t)^2 t\\mathbf{P}_1 + (1 - t)t^2 \\mathbf{P}_2 + (1 - t)^{2}t\\mathbf{P}_0 + 2(1 - t)t^2 \\mathbf{P}_1 + t^3 \\mathbf{P}_2 \\\\\n  &amp;= (1 - t)^3 \\mathbf{P}_0 + (1 - t)^2 t \\left( \\mathbf{P}_0 + 2\\mathbf{P}_1\\right) + (1 - t) t^2 \\left(2\\mathbf{P}_1 + \\mathbf{P}_2\\right) + t^{3}\\mathbf{P}_2 \\\\\n  &amp;= (1 - t)^3 \\mathbf{P}_0 + 3(1 - t)^2 t \\tfrac{1}{3} \\left( \\mathbf{P}_0 + 2\\mathbf{P}_1 \\right) + 3(1 - t) t^2 \\tfrac{1}{3} \\left( 2\\mathbf{P}_1 + \\mathbf{P}_2 \\right) + t^{3}\\mathbf{P}_2\n\\end{align}&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}(1-t)^{2}\mathbf {P} _{0}+2(1-t)t\mathbf {P} _{1}+t^{2}\mathbf {P} _{2}&=(1-t)^{3}\mathbf {P} _{0}+2(1-t)^{2}t\mathbf {P} _{1}+(1-t)t^{2}\mathbf {P} _{2}+(1-t)^{2}t\mathbf {P} _{0}+2(1-t)t^{2}\mathbf {P} _{1}+t^{3}\mathbf {P} _{2}\\&=(1-t)^{3}\mathbf {P} _{0}+(1-t)^{2}t\left(\mathbf {P} _{0}+2\mathbf {P} _{1}\right)+(1-t)t^{2}\left(2\mathbf {P} _{1}+\mathbf {P} _{2}\right)+t^{3}\mathbf {P} _{2}\\&=(1-t)^{3}\mathbf {P} _{0}+3(1-t)^{2}t{\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(\mathbf {P} _{0}+2\mathbf {P} _{1}\right)+3(1-t)t^{2}{\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(2\mathbf {P} _{1}+\mathbf {P} _{2}\right)+t^{3}\mathbf {P} _{2}\end{aligned}}}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/87e5d723b13959c2542c190ba4f3a6f5ab083950"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -4.505ex; width:114.022ex; height:10.176ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}(1-t)^{2}\mathbf {P} _{0}+2(1-t)t\mathbf {P} _{1}+t^{2}\mathbf {P} _{2}&amp;=(1-t)^{3}\mathbf {P} _{0}+2(1-t)^{2}t\mathbf {P} _{1}+(1-t)t^{2}\mathbf {P} _{2}+(1-t)^{2}t\mathbf {P} _{0}+2(1-t)t^{2}\mathbf {P} _{1}+t^{3}\mathbf {P} _{2}\\&amp;=(1-t)^{3}\mathbf {P} _{0}+(1-t)^{2}t\left(\mathbf {P} _{0}+2\mathbf {P} _{1}\right)+(1-t)t^{2}\left(2\mathbf {P} _{1}+\mathbf {P} _{2}\right)+t^{3}\mathbf {P} _{2}\\&amp;=(1-t)^{3}\mathbf {P} _{0}+3(1-t)^{2}t{\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(\mathbf {P} _{0}+2\mathbf {P} _{1}\right)+3(1-t)t^{2}{\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(2\mathbf {P} _{1}+\mathbf {P} _{2}\right)+t^{3}\mathbf {P} _{2}\end{aligned}}}" /></span>

In other words, the original start and end points are unchanged. The new
control points are <span id="mwAs8"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt121"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{P'}_1 = \\tfrac{1}{3} \\left( \\mathbf{P}_0 + 2\\mathbf{P}_1 \\right)&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {P'} _{1}={\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(\mathbf {P} _{0}+2\mathbf {P} _{1}\right)}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/31d841209e52ac70940e7fe3c332af9226f9f183"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -1.338ex; width:20.283ex; height:3.676ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {P&#39;} _{1}={\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(\mathbf {P} _{0}+2\mathbf {P} _{1}\right)}" /></span>
and <span id="mwAtA" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt122"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{P'}_2 = \\tfrac{1}{3} \\left( 2\\mathbf{P}_1 + \\mathbf{P}_2 \\right)&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {P'} _{2}={\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(2\mathbf {P} _{1}+\mathbf {P} _{2}\right)}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c939376bd4da5d4efd2393a438a4771b5e207b70"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -1.338ex; width:20.283ex; height:3.676ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {P&#39;} _{2}={\tfrac {1}{3}}\left(2\mathbf {P} _{1}+\mathbf {P} _{2}\right)}" /></span>.

For arbitrary *n* we use
equalities<sup><a href="#cite_note-farin1997-22" id="mwAtM"><span id="mwAtQ"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAtU"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>20<span id="mwAtY"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

<span id="mwAtk" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt124"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\begin{cases} {n + 1 \\choose i}(1 - t)\\mathbf{b}_{i, n} = {n \\choose i} \\mathbf{b}_{i, n + 1} \\\\ {n + 1 \\choose i + 1} t\\mathbf{b}_{i, n} = {n \\choose i} \\mathbf{b}_{i + 1, n + 1} \\end{cases} \\quad \\implies \\quad \\begin{cases} (1 - t)\\mathbf{b}_{i, n} = \\frac{n + 1 - i}{n + 1} \\mathbf{b}_{i, n + 1} \\\\ t\\mathbf{b}_{i, n} = \\frac{i + 1}{n + 1} \\mathbf{b}_{i + 1, n + 1} \\end{cases} &quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle {\begin{cases}{n+1 \choose i}(1-t)\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={n \choose i}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}\\{n+1 \choose i+1}t\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={n \choose i}\mathbf {b} _{i+1,n+1}\end{cases}}\quad \implies \quad {\begin{cases}(1-t)\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}\\t\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={\frac {i+1}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i+1,n+1}\end{cases}}}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f0d7c85c716928f3456fedec7ade3628d40a7f5c"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -3.338ex; width:68.998ex; height:7.843ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle {\begin{cases}{n+1 \choose i}(1-t)\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={n \choose i}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}\\{n+1 \choose i+1}t\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={n \choose i}\mathbf {b} _{i+1,n+1}\end{cases}}\quad \implies \quad {\begin{cases}(1-t)\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}\\t\mathbf {b} _{i,n}={\frac {i+1}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i+1,n+1}\end{cases}}}" /></span>

Therefore:

<span id="mwAt0" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt125"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\begin{align}\n\\mathbf{B}(t) &amp;= (1 - t)\\sum_{i=0}^n \\mathbf{b}_{i, n}(t)\\mathbf{P}_i + t\\sum_{i=0}^n \\mathbf{b}_{i, n}(t)\\mathbf{P}_i \\\\\n                &amp;= \\sum_{i=0}^n \\frac{n + 1 - i}{n + 1}\\mathbf{b}_{i, n + 1}(t)\\mathbf{P}_i + \\sum_{i=0}^n \\frac{i + 1}{n + 1}\\mathbf{b}_{i + 1, n + 1}(t)\\mathbf{P}_i \\\\\n                &amp;= \\sum_{i=0}^{n + 1} \\left(\\frac{i}{n + 1}\\mathbf{P}_{i - 1} + \\frac{n + 1 - i}{n + 1}\\mathbf{P}_i\\right) \\mathbf{b}_{i, n + 1}(t) \\\\\n                &amp;= \\sum_{i=0}^{n + 1} \\mathbf{b}_{i, n + 1}(t)\\mathbf{P'}_i\n\\end{align}&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\mathbf {B} (t)&=(1-t)\sum _{i=0}^{n}\mathbf {b} _{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}+t\sum _{i=0}^{n}\mathbf {b} _{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}\\&=\sum _{i=0}^{n}{\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}+\sum _{i=0}^{n}{\frac {i+1}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i+1,n+1}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}\\&=\sum _{i=0}^{n+1}\left({\frac {i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i-1}+{\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i}\right)\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}(t)\\&=\sum _{i=0}^{n+1}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}(t)\mathbf {P'} _{i}\end{aligned}}}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/515e443bc4f62181ba4f94729b2457c25eee75d4"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -14.005ex; width:59.144ex; height:29.176ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\mathbf {B} (t)&amp;=(1-t)\sum _{i=0}^{n}\mathbf {b} _{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}+t\sum _{i=0}^{n}\mathbf {b} _{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}\\&amp;=\sum _{i=0}^{n}{\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}+\sum _{i=0}^{n}{\frac {i+1}{n+1}}\mathbf {b} _{i+1,n+1}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}\\&amp;=\sum _{i=0}^{n+1}\left({\frac {i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i-1}+{\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i}\right)\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}(t)\\&amp;=\sum _{i=0}^{n+1}\mathbf {b} _{i,n+1}(t)\mathbf {P&#39;} _{i}\end{aligned}}}" /></span>

introducing arbitrary <span id="mwAt8"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt126"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{P}_{-1}&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {P} _{-1}}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/93b128babf6e1c73396f382c443369df678dbd69"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.671ex; width:4.16ex; height:2.509ex;"
aria-hidden="true" alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {P} _{-1}}" /></span> and
<span id="mwAuA" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt127"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{P}_{n + 1}&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {P} _{n+1}}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a3c528f8bbfc45d6be1cc2446f32b5d4409c2308"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.671ex; width:5.146ex; height:2.509ex;"
aria-hidden="true" alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {P} _{n+1}}" /></span>.

Therefore, new control points
are<sup><a href="#cite_note-farin1997-22" id="mwAuI"><span id="mwAuM"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAuQ"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>20<span id="mwAuU"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

<span id="mwAug" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt131"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{P'}_i = \\frac{i}{n + 1}\\mathbf{P}_{i - 1} + \\frac{n + 1 - i}{n + 1}\\mathbf{P}_i,\\quad i=0, \\ldots, n + 1.&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {P'} _{i}={\frac {i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i-1}+{\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i},\quad i=0,\ldots ,n+1.}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3d8155f96d25413886ddb44a1f6c18193a8ddc0e"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -2.005ex; width:52.356ex; height:5.343ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {P&#39;} _{i}={\frac {i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i-1}+{\frac {n+1-i}{n+1}}\mathbf {P} _{i},\quad i=0,\ldots ,n+1.}" /></span>

<div id="mwAuk" class="section" mw-section-id="20"
aria-labelledby="Repeated_degree_elevation">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Repeated degree elevation

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Repeated degree elevation")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

The concept of degree elevation can be repeated on a control polygon
**R** to get a sequence of control polygons **R**, **R**<sub>1</sub>,
**R**<sub>2</sub>, and so on. After *r* degree elevations, the polygon
**R**<sub>*r*</sub> has the vertices **P**<sub>0,*r*</sub>,
**P**<sub>1,*r*</sub>, **P**<sub>2,*r*</sub>, ...,
**P**<sub>*n*+*r*,*r*</sub> given by
<sup><a href="#cite_note-farin1997-22" id="mwAwM"><span id="mwAwQ"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAwU"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>20<span id="mwAwY"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

<span id="mwAwk" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt133"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{P}_{i,r} = \\sum_{j=0}^n \\mathbf{P}_j \\tbinom nj \\frac{\\tbinom{r}{i-j}}{\\tbinom{n+r}{i}}.&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {P} _{i,r}=\sum _{j=0}^{n}\mathbf {P} _{j}{\tbinom {n}{j}}{\frac {\tbinom {r}{i-j}}{\tbinom {n+r}{i}}}.}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e0d594c365e3e16a5f13b46d3d91cc5dc0e44800"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -3.338ex; width:23.137ex; height:7.843ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {P} _{i,r}=\sum _{j=0}^{n}\mathbf {P} _{j}{\tbinom {n}{j}}{\frac {\tbinom {r}{i-j}}{\tbinom {n+r}{i}}}.}" /></span>

It can also be shown that for the underlying Bézier curve *B*,

<span id="mwAw4" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt134"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{\\lim_{r \\to \\infty}R_r} = \\mathbf{B}.&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {\lim _{r\to \infty }R_{r}} =\mathbf {B} .}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9eabbb4d5b7254a5b9e596c2e9668ed9a59dba10"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -1.838ex; width:13.114ex; height:3.676ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {\lim _{r\to \infty }R_{r}} =\mathbf {B} .}" /></span>

</div>

<div id="mwAw8" class="section" mw-section-id="21"
aria-labelledby="Degree_reduction">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Degree reduction

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section: Degree reduction")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

Degree reduction can only be done exactly when the curve in question is
originally elevated from a lower
degree.<sup><a href="#cite_note-ffbezier-23" id="mwAxE"><span id="mwAxI"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAxM"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>21<span id="mwAxQ"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> A number of approximation
algorithms have been proposed and used in
practice.<sup><a href="#cite_note-24" id="mwAxU"><span id="mwAxY"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAxc"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>22<span id="mwAxg"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a><a href="#cite_note-25" id="mwAxk"><span id="mwAxo"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAxs"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>23<span id="mwAxw"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

</div>

</div>

<div id="mwAx0" class="section" mw-section-id="22"
aria-labelledby="Rational_Bézier_curves">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2">

## <span id="Rational_B.C3.A9zier_curves" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Rational Bézier curves

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Rational Bézier curves")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

<figure id="mwAx4" class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb">
<a
href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rational_Bezier_curve-conic_sections.svg"
id="mwAx8" class="mw-file-description"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Rational_Bezier_curve-conic_sections.svg/250px-Rational_Bezier_curve-conic_sections.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"
id="mwAyA" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rational_Bezier_curve-conic_sections.svg"
decoding="async" data-file-width="380" data-file-height="290"
data-file-type="drawing"
srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Rational_Bezier_curve-conic_sections.svg/500px-Rational_Bezier_curve-conic_sections.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x"
width="250" height="191" /></a>
<figcaption>Segments of conic sections represented exactly by rational
Bézier curves</figcaption>
</figure>

The rational Bézier curve adds adjustable weights to provide closer
approximations to arbitrary shapes. The numerator is a weighted
Bernstein-form Bézier curve and the denominator is a weighted sum of
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernstein_polynomial" id="mwAyM"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Bernstein polynomial">Bernstein polynomials</a>.
Rational Bézier curves can, among other uses, be used to represent
segments of
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conic_section" id="mwAyQ"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Conic section">conic sections</a> exactly,
including circular
arcs.<sup><a href="#cite_note-26" id="mwAyU"><span id="mwAyY"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAyc"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>24<span id="mwAyg"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

Given *n*<span id="mwAys" typeof="mw:Entity"> </span>+ 1 control points
**P**<sub>0</sub>, ..., **P**<sub>*n*</sub>, the rational Bézier curve
can be described by

<span id="mwAzM" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt147"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{B}(t) = \\frac{\n\\sum_{i=0}^n b_{i,n}(t) \\mathbf{P}_{i}w_i\n}\n{\n\\sum_{i=0}^n b_{i,n}(t) w_i\n},&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {B} (t)={\frac {\sum _{i=0}^{n}b_{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}w_{i}}{\sum _{i=0}^{n}b_{i,n}(t)w_{i}}},}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/deae2bdb4b272a77b902cbce78d51c9aff26ad8d"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -2.838ex; width:25.852ex; height:6.843ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {B} (t)={\frac {\sum _{i=0}^{n}b_{i,n}(t)\mathbf {P} _{i}w_{i}}{\sum _{i=0}^{n}b_{i,n}(t)w_{i}}},}" /></span>

or simply

<span id="mwAzc" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt148"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;\\mathbf{B}(t) = \\frac{\n\\sum_{i=0}^n {n \\choose i} t^i (1-t)^{n-i}\\mathbf{P}_{i}w_i\n}\n{\n\\sum_{i=0}^n {n \\choose i} t^i (1-t)^{n-i}w_i\n}.&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle \mathbf {B} (t)={\frac {\sum _{i=0}^{n}{n \choose i}t^{i}(1-t)^{n-i}\mathbf {P} _{i}w_{i}}{\sum _{i=0}^{n}{n \choose i}t^{i}(1-t)^{n-i}w_{i}}}.}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a36b6a4a5fc8defc6af38d016596253b8d5f628c"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -3.171ex; width:34.434ex; height:7.509ex;"
aria-hidden="true"
alt="{\displaystyle \mathbf {B} (t)={\frac {\sum _{i=0}^{n}{n \choose i}t^{i}(1-t)^{n-i}\mathbf {P} _{i}w_{i}}{\sum _{i=0}^{n}{n \choose i}t^{i}(1-t)^{n-i}w_{i}}}.}" /></span>

The expression can be extended by using number systems besides
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_number" id="mwAzk"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Real number">reals</a> for the weights. In the
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_plane" id="mwAzo"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Complex plane">complex plane</a> the points
{1}, {-1}, and {1} with weights {<span id="mwAzs"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt149"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;i&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle i}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/add78d8608ad86e54951b8c8bd6c8d8416533d20"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.338ex; width:0.802ex; height:2.176ex;"
aria-hidden="true" alt="{\displaystyle i}" /></span>}, {1}, and
{<span id="mwAzw" class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt150"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;-i&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle -i}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/91fddb9f89a520937db3a8821575068cdcc76f60"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.505ex; width:2.611ex; height:2.343ex;"
aria-hidden="true" alt="{\displaystyle -i}" /></span>} generate a full
circle with radius one. For curves with points and weights on a circle,
the weights can be scaled without changing the curve's
shape.<sup><a href="#cite_note-27" id="mwAz0"><span id="mwAz4"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwAz8"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>25<span id="mwA0A"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> Scaling the central
weight of the above curve by 1.35508 gives a more uniform
parameterization.

</div>

<div id="mwA0E" class="section" mw-section-id="23"
aria-labelledby="Applications">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2">

## Applications

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section: Applications")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

<div id="mwA0I" class="section" mw-section-id="24"
aria-labelledby="Computer_graphics">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Computer graphics

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Computer graphics")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

<figure id="mwA0M" class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left"
typeof="mw:File/Thumb">
<a
href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_curve_in_Adobe_Illustrator_CS2.png"
id="mwA0Q" class="mw-file-description"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/b/b8/B%C3%A9zier_curve_in_Adobe_Illustrator_CS2.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled"
id="mwA0U" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bézier_curve_in_Adobe_Illustrator_CS2.png"
decoding="async" data-file-width="119" data-file-height="96"
data-file-type="bitmap" width="119" height="96" /></a>
<figcaption>Bézier path in <a
href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adobe_Illustrator" id="mwA0c"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Adobe Illustrator">Adobe
Illustrator</a></figcaption>
</figure>

Bézier curves are widely used in computer graphics to model smooth
curves. As the curve is completely contained in the
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convex_hull" id="mwA0k"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Convex hull">convex hull</a> of its
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_point_(mathematics)"
id="mwA0o" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Control point (mathematics)">control
points</a>, the points can be graphically displayed and used to
manipulate the curve intuitively.
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affine_transformation" id="mwA0s"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Affine transformation">Affine
transformations</a> such as
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Translation_(geometry)"
id="mwA0w" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Translation (geometry)">translation</a> and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotation" id="mwA00"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Rotation">rotation</a> can be applied on the
curve by applying the respective transform on the control points of the
curve.

<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadratic_function" id="mwA08"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Quadratic function">Quadratic</a> and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubic_function" id="mwA1A"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Cubic function">cubic</a> Bézier curves are
most common. Higher degree curves are more
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computationally_expensive"
id="mwA1E" class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Computationally expensive">computationally expensive</a> to
evaluate. When more complex shapes are needed, low order Bézier curves
are patched together, producing a
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Composite_Bézier_curve"
id="mwA1I" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Composite Bézier curve">composite
Bézier curve</a>. A composite Bézier curve is commonly referred to as a
"path" in
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vector_graphics" id="mwA1M"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Vector graphics">vector graphics</a> languages
(like <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PostScript" id="mwA1Q"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="PostScript">PostScript</a>), vector graphics
standards (like
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scalable_vector_graphics"
id="mwA1U" class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Scalable vector graphics">SVG</a>) and vector graphics programs
(like
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artline_(program)" id="mwA1Y"
class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Artline (program)">Artline</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeworks_Publisher" id="mwA1c"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Timeworks Publisher">Timeworks Publisher</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adobe_Illustrator" id="mwA1g"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Adobe Illustrator">Adobe Illustrator</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CorelDraw" id="mwA1k"
class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="CorelDraw">CorelDraw</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inkscape" id="mwA1o"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Inkscape">Inkscape</a>, and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegro_(software_library)"
id="mwA1s" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Allegro (software library)">Allegro</a>). In order to join Bézier
curves into a composite Bézier curve without kinks, a property called
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoothness#Geometric_continuity"
id="mwA1w" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Smoothness"><em>G1
continuity</em></a> suffices to force the control point at which two
constituent Bézier curves meet to lie on the line defined by the two
control points on either side.

<figure id="mwA14" class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb">
<a
href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bezier_curves_composition_ray-traced_in_3D.png"
id="mwA18" class="mw-file-description"><img
src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bezier_curves_composition_ray-traced_in_3D.png/250px-Bezier_curves_composition_ray-traced_in_3D.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail"
id="mwA2A" class="mw-file-element"
resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bezier_curves_composition_ray-traced_in_3D.png"
decoding="async" data-file-width="1936" data-file-height="1936"
data-file-type="bitmap"
srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Bezier_curves_composition_ray-traced_in_3D.png/500px-Bezier_curves_composition_ray-traced_in_3D.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x"
width="250" height="250" /></a>
<figcaption>Abstract composition of cubic Bézier curves ray-traced in
3D. Ray intersection with swept volumes along curves is calculated with
Phantom Ray-Hair Intersector algorithm.<sup><a href="#cite_note-28"
id="mwA2I"><span id="mwA2M" class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA2Q"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>26<span id="mwA2U"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup></figcaption>
</figure>

The simplest method for scan converting
(<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasterisation" id="mwA2c"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Rasterisation">rasterizing</a>) a Bézier curve
is to evaluate it at many closely spaced points and scan convert the
approximating sequence of line segments. However, this does not
guarantee that the rasterized output looks sufficiently smooth, because
the points may be spaced too far apart. Conversely it may generate too
many points in areas where the curve is close to linear. A common
adaptive method is recursive subdivision, in which a curve's control
points are checked to see if the curve approximates a line to within a
small tolerance. If not, the curve is subdivided parametrically into two
segments, 0 ≤ *t* ≤ 0.5 and 0.5 ≤ *t* ≤ 1, and the same procedure is
applied recursively to each half. There are also forward differencing
methods, but great care must be taken to analyse error
propagation.<sup><a href="#cite_note-Xuexiang-29" id="mwA2o"><span id="mwA2s"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA2w"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>27<span id="mwA20"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

Analytical methods where a Bézier is intersected with each
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scan_line" id="mwA28"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Scan line">scan line</a> involve finding
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_of_a_function" id="mwA3A"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Zero of a function">roots</a> of cubic
polynomials (for cubic Béziers) and dealing with multiple roots, so they
are not often used in
practice.<sup><a href="#cite_note-Xuexiang-29" id="mwA3E"><span id="mwA3I"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA3M"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>27<span id="mwA3Q"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

The rasterisation algorithm used in
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metafont" id="mwA3Y"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Metafont">Metafont</a> is based on discretising
the curve, so that it is approximated by a sequence of
"<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rook_(chess)" id="mwA3c"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Rook (chess)">rook</a> moves" that are purely
vertical or purely horizontal, along the
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixel" id="mwA3g"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Pixel">pixel</a> boundaries. To that end, the
plane is first split into eight 45° sectors (by the coordinate axes and
the two lines <span id="mwA3k"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt159"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot; y = \\pm x &quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle y=\pm x}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2518154984c3e8016d09fec4ca47ed077c9424cd"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.671ex; width:7.392ex; height:2.509ex;"
aria-hidden="true" alt="{\displaystyle y=\pm x}" /></span>), then the
curve is decomposed into smaller segments such that the *direction* of a
curve segment stays within one sector; since the curve velocity is a
second degree polynomial, finding the <span id="mwA3s"
class="mwe-math-element mwe-math-element-inline"
typeof="mw:Extension/math" about="#mwt160"
mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;math&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;t&quot;}}"><span class="mwe-math-mathml-inline mwe-math-mathml-a11y"
style="display: none;">$`{\displaystyle t}`$</span><img
src="https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/65658b7b223af9e1acc877d848888ecdb4466560"
class="mwe-math-fallback-image-inline mw-invert skin-invert"
style="vertical-align: -0.338ex; width:0.84ex; height:2.009ex;"
aria-hidden="true" alt="{\displaystyle t}" /></span> values where it is
parallel to one of these lines can be done by solving
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quadratic_equation" id="mwA3w"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Quadratic equation">quadratic equations</a>.
Within each segment, either horizontal or vertical movement dominates,
and the total number of steps in either direction can be read off from
the endpoint coordinates; in for example the 0–45° sector horizontal
movement to the right dominates, so it only remains to decide between
which steps to the right the curve should make a step
up.<sup><a href="#cite_note-30" id="mwA30"><span id="mwA34"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA38"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>28<span id="mwA4A"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

There is also a modified curve form of
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bresenham&#39;s_line_algorithm"
id="mwA4I" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Bresenham&#39;s line algorithm">Bresenham's line drawing
algorithm</a> by Zingl that performs this rasterization by subdividing
the curve into rational pieces and calculating the error at each pixel
location such that it either travels at a 45° angle or straight
depending on compounding error as it iterates through the curve. This
reduces the next step calculation to a series of
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integer" id="mwA4M"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Integer">integer</a> additions and
subtractions.<sup><a href="#cite_note-Zingl-31" id="mwA4Q"><span id="mwA4U"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA4Y"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>29<span id="mwA4c"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

</div>

<div id="mwA4g" class="section" mw-section-id="25"
aria-labelledby="Animation">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Animation

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Animation")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

In animation applications, such as
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adobe_Flash" id="mwA4o"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Adobe Flash">Adobe Flash</a> and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synfig" id="mwA4s"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Synfig">Synfig</a>, Bézier curves are used to
outline, for example, movement. Users outline the wanted path in Bézier
curves, and the application creates the needed frames for the object to
move along the
path.<sup><a href="#cite_note-32" id="mwA4w"><span id="mwA40"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA44"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>30<span id="mwA48"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a><a href="#cite_note-33" id="mwA5A"><span id="mwA5E"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA5I"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>31<span id="mwA5M"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

In 3D animation, Bézier curves are often used to define 3D paths as well
as 2D curves for keyframe
interpolation.<sup><a href="#cite_note-34" id="mwA5U"><span id="mwA5Y"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA5c"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>32<span id="mwA5g"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> Bézier curves are now
very frequently used to control the animation easing in
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSS" id="mwA5k" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="CSS">CSS</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JavaScript" id="mwA5o"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="JavaScript">JavaScript</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JavaFx" id="mwA5s"
class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="JavaFx">JavaFx</a> and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flutter_(software)" id="mwA5w"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Flutter (software)">Flutter SDK</a>.<sup><a href="#cite_note-:0-5" id="mwA50"><span id="mwA54"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA58"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>4<span id="mwA6A"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

</div>

<div id="mwA6E" class="section" mw-section-id="26"
aria-labelledby="Fonts">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Fonts

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Fonts")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TrueType" id="mwA6M"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="TrueType">TrueType</a> fonts use composite
Bézier curves composed of quadratic Bézier curves. Other languages and
imaging tools (such as
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PostScript" id="mwA6Q"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="PostScript">PostScript</a>, <a
href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asymptote_(vector_graphics_language)"
id="mwA6U" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Asymptote (vector graphics language)">Asymptote</a>,
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metafont" id="mwA6Y"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Metafont">Metafont</a>, and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scalable_vector_graphics"
id="mwA6c" class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Scalable vector graphics">SVG</a>) use composite Béziers composed
of cubic Bézier curves for drawing curved shapes.
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenType" id="mwA6g"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="OpenType">OpenType</a> fonts can use either
kind of curve, depending on which font technology underlies the OpenType
wrapper.<sup><a href="#cite_note-35" id="mwA6k"><span id="mwA6o"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA6s"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>33<span id="mwA6w"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

Font engines, like
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FreeType" id="mwA64"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="FreeType">FreeType</a>, draw the font's curves
(and lines) on a pixellated surface using a process known as
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Font_rasterization" id="mwA68"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Font rasterization">font rasterization</a>.<sup><a href="#cite_note-freetype-15" id="mwA7A"><span id="mwA7E"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA7I"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>13<span id="mwA7M"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> Typically font engines
and vector graphics engines render Bézier curves by splitting them
recursively up to the point where the curve is flat enough to be drawn
as a series of linear or circular segments. The exact splitting
algorithm is implementation dependent, only the flatness criteria must
be respected to reach the necessary precision and to avoid non-monotonic
local changes of curvature. The "smooth curve" feature of charts in
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft_Excel" id="mwA7Q"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Microsoft Excel">Microsoft Excel</a> also uses
this algorithm.<sup><a href="#cite_note-36" id="mwA7U"><span id="mwA7Y"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA7c"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>34<span id="mwA7g"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

Because arcs of circles and
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellipse" id="mwA7o"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Ellipse">ellipses</a> cannot be exactly
represented by Bézier curves, they are first approximated by Bézier
curves, which are in turn approximated by arcs of circles. This is
inefficient as there exists also approximations of all Bézier curves
using arcs of circles or ellipses, which can be rendered incrementally
with arbitrary precision. Another approach, used by modern hardware
graphics adapters with accelerated geometry, can convert exactly all
Bézier and conic curves (or surfaces) into
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-uniform_rational_B-spline"
id="mwA7s" rel="mw:WikiLink"
title="Non-uniform rational B-spline">NURBS</a>, that can be rendered
incrementally without first splitting the curve recursively to reach the
necessary flatness condition. This approach also preserves the curve
definition under all linear or perspective 2D and 3D transforms and
projections.<sup><span typeof="mw:Entity">\[</span>*<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed"
rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span
title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2015)">citation
needed</span></a>*<span typeof="mw:Entity">\]</span></sup>

</div>

<div id="mwA74" class="section" mw-section-id="27"
aria-labelledby="Robotics">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading3">

### Robotics

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: Robotics")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

Because the control polygon allows to tell whether or not the path
collides with any obstacles, Bézier curves are used in producing
trajectories of the
<a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/End_effector" id="mwA8A"
class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="End effector">end
effectors</a>.<sup><a href="#cite_note-Malik2021-37" id="mwA8E"><span id="mwA8I"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA8M"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>35<span id="mwA8Q"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup> Furthermore, joint space
trajectories can be accurately differentiated using Bézier curves.
Consequently, the derivatives of joint space trajectories are used in
the calculation of the dynamics and control effort (torque profiles) of
the robotic
manipulator.<sup><a href="#cite_note-Malik2021-37" id="mwA8U"><span id="mwA8Y"
class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA8c"
class="cite-bracket">[</span>35<span id="mwA8g"
class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup>

</div>

</div>

<div id="mwA8k" class="section" mw-section-id="28"
aria-labelledby="See_also">

<div class="mw-heading mw-heading2">

## See also

<span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\[</span>[edit](/w/index.php?title=B%C3%A9zier_curve&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: See also")<span class="mw-editsection-bracket">\]</span></span>

</div>

- <span id="mwA8s"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bézier_surface" id="mwA8w"
  rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Bézier surface">Bézier surface</a></span>
- <span id="mwA80"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B-spline" id="mwA84"
  rel="mw:WikiLink" title="B-spline">B-spline</a></span>
- <span id="mwA88"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GEM/4" id="mwA9A"
  class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="GEM/4">GEM/4</a> and
  <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GEM/5" id="mwA9E"
  class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="GEM/5">GEM/5</a></span>
- <span id="mwA9I"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermite_curve" id="mwA9M"
  class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Hermite curve">Hermite
  curve</a></span>
- <span id="mwA9Q"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integral_curve" id="mwA9U"
  rel="mw:WikiLink" title="Integral curve">Integral curve</a></span>
- <span id="mwA9Y"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NURBS" id="mwA9c"
  class="mw-redirect" rel="mw:WikiLink" title="NURBS">NURBS</a></span>
- <span id="mwA9g"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/String_art" id="mwA9k"
  rel="mw:WikiLink" title="String art">String art</a> – Bézier curves
  are also formed by many common forms of string art, where strings are
  looped across a frame of
  nails.<sup><a href="#cite_note-38" id="mwA9o"><span id="mwA9s"
  class="mw-reflink-text"><span id="mwA9w"
  class="cite-bracket">[</span>36<span id="mwA90"
  class="cite-bracket">]</span></span></a></sup></span>
- <span id="mwA94"><a

    class="mw-linkback-text">↑</span></a></span>
    <span id="mw-reference-text-cite_note-35"
    class="mw-reference-text reference-text"><a
    href="https://web.archive.org/web/20170703232935/https://www.linotype.com/8120/the-difference-between-cff-and-ttf.html"
    id="mwBX4" class="external text" rel="mw:ExtLink nofollow">"The
    difference between CFF and TTF"</a>. *Know How*. Linotype. Archived
    from <a
    href="https://www.linotype.com/8120/the-difference-between-cff-and-ttf.html"
    id="mwBYA" class="external text" rel="mw:ExtLink nofollow">the
    original</a> on 2017-07-03<span id="mwBYE"
    class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span id="mwBYI"
    class="nowrap">3 July</span> 2018</span>. “The OpenType format was
    formulated in 1996. By 2003, it began to replace two competing
    formats: the Type1 fonts, developed by Adobe and based on
    \[P\]ost\[S\]cript, and the TrueType fonts, specified by Microsoft
    and Apple. (...) TTF stands for TrueTypeFont and indicates that the
    font data is the same as in the TrueType fonts. CFF stands for the
    Type1 font format. Strictly speaking, it refers to the Compact Font
    Format, which is used in the compression processes for the Type2
    fonts. (...) the cubic Bézier format of the Type1 fonts is more
    space-saving compared to the quadratic format of the TrueType fonts.
    Some kilobytes can be saved in large, elaborate fonts which may
    represent an advantage on the Web. On the other hand, the more
    detailed hinting information of the TrueType fonts is useful for
    very extensive optimization for screen use.”<span id="mwBYQ"
    class="Z3988"
    title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=Know+How&amp;rft.atitle=The+difference+between+CFF+and+TTF&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.linotype.com%2F8120%2Fthe-difference-between-cff-and-ttf.html&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AB%C3%A9zier+curve"
    about="#mwt179"></span></span></span>
